=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
Alice and Bob are playing a fantasy battle game consisting of n rounds where they summon one of three magical creatures each round: a Fire Dragon, a Water Serpent, or an Earth Golem. In each round, players simultaneously summon their creature and are awarded points as follows:

If one player summons a Fire Dragon and the other summons an Earth Golem, the player who summoned the Fire Dragon is awarded a point.
If one player summons a Water Serpent and the other summons a Fire Dragon, the player who summoned the Water Serpent is awarded a point.
If one player summons an Earth Golem and the other summons a Water Serpent, the player who summoned the Earth Golem is awarded a point.
If both players summon the same creature, no player is awarded a point.

You are given a string s consisting of n characters 'F', 'W', and 'E', representing the sequence of creatures Alice will summon in each round:

If s[i] == 'F', Alice summons a Fire Dragon.
If s[i] == 'W', Alice summons a Water Serpent.
If s[i] == 'E', Alice summons an Earth Golem.

Bob’s sequence of moves is unknown, but it is guaranteed that Bob will never summon the same creature in two consecutive rounds. Bob beats Alice if the total number of points awarded to Bob after n rounds is strictly greater than the points awarded to Alice.
Return the number of distinct sequences Bob can use to beat Alice.
Since the answer may be very large, return it modulo 10^9 + 7.
 
Example 1:

Input: s = "FFF"
Output: 3
Explanation:
Bob can beat Alice by making one of the following sequences of moves: "WFW", "FWF", or "WEW". Note that other winning sequences like "WWE" or "EWW" are invalid since Bob cannot make the same move twice in a row.

Example 2:

Input: s = "FWEFW"
Output: 18
Explanation:
Bob can beat Alice by making one of the following sequences of moves: "FWFWF", "FWFWE", "FWEFE", "FWEWE", "FEFWF", "FEFWE", "FEFEW", "FEWFE", "WFEFE", "WFEWE", "WEFWF", "WEFWE", "WEFEF", "WEFEW", "WEWFW", "WEWFE", "EWFWE", or "EWEWE".

 
Constraints:

1 <= s.length <= 1000
s[i] is one of 'F', 'W', or 'E'.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def countWinningSequences(self, s: str) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Pseudo:

class Solution:
    def countWinningSequences(self, s: str) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        n = len(s)
        moves = ['F','W','E']
        inc = {
            'F': [0, 1, -1],
            'W': [-1, 0, 1],
            'E': [1, -1, 0]
        }
        shift = n
        size = 2*n + 3
        dp = [[0]*size for _ in range(3)]
        first_incs = inc[s[0]]
        for b in range(3):
            dp[b][shift + first_incs[b]] = 1

        for ch in s[1:]:
            incs = inc[ch]
            ndp = [[0]*size for _ in range(3)]
            for last in range(3):
                arr = dp[last]
                # We can loop score from 0 to size-1
                for sc in range(size):
                    val = arr[sc]
                    if val:
                        # next moves not last
                        if last == 0:
                            # nxt 1,2
                            ns = sc + incs[1]; ndp[1][ns] = (ndp[1][ns] + val) % MOD
                            ns = sc + incs[2]; ndp[2][ns] = (ndp[2][ns] + val) % MOD
                        elif last == 1:
                            ns = sc + incs[0]; ndp[0][ns] = (ndp[0][ns] + val) % MOD
                            ns = sc + incs[2]; ndp[2][ns] = (ndp[2][ns] + val) % MOD
                        else:
                            ns = sc + incs[0]; ndp[0][ns] = (ndp[0][ns] + val) % MOD
                            ns = sc + incs[1]; ndp[1][ns] = (ndp[1][ns] + val) % MOD
            dp = ndp

        ans = 0
        for b in range(3):
            ans = (ans + sum(dp[b][shift+1:])) % MOD
        return ans

F 1 c1 -> next W 1, next E 2.

W -1 c1 -> next F score -2, next E score 0.
W 0 c1 -> next F -1, next E 1.
W 1 c3 -> next F 0 (3), next E 2 (3).
W 2 c1 -> next F 1, next E 3.
W 3 c1 -> next F 2, next E 4.
W 4 c1 -> next F 3, next E 5.

From E states:
E -4 c1 -> next F score -5, next W score -4.
E -2 c1 -> next F -3, next W -2.

E 0 c2 -> next F -1 (2), next W 0 (2).
E 1 c1 -> next F 0, next W 1.
E 2 c1 -> next F 1, next W 2.

Now aggregate final positive scores >0 by last.

From W0: -1

From W2: 1
From W3: 2
From W4: 3

From E0 c2: -1 (2) (F -1 total from W0 1 + E0 2 =3)
From E1: 0 (1) (F0 total 3+1=4)
From E2: 1 (1) (F1 total 1+1=2)
Final F positive: score1 count2, score2 count1, score3 count1. Total F positive =4.

Final W scores:
From F -3: -3
From F -2: -2
From F -1 c2: -1 (2)
From F0 c3: 0 (3)
From F1: 1

From E1: 1 (1) (W1 total 1+1=2)
From E2: 2
Final W positive: score1 count2, score2 count1. Total W positive=3.

From F -3: -2

From W2: 3
From W3: 4
From W4: 5
Final E positive: score1 count4, score2 count4, score3 count1, score4 count1, score5 count1. Total E positive=11.

Let's maybe write final code:

```python
class Solution:
    def countWinningSequences(self, s: str) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        n = len(s)
        shift = n
        size = 2 * n + 3
        
        inc = {
            'F': [0, 1, -1],
            'W': [-1, 0, 1],
            'E': [1, -1, 0],
        }
        nxt = ((1, 2), (0, 2), (0, 1))
        
        dp = [[0] * size for _ in range(3)]
        first = inc[s[0]]
        for m in range(3):
            dp[m][shift + first[m]] = 1
        
        for ch in s[1:]:
            cur = inc[ch]
            ndp = [[0] * size for _ in range(3)]
            for last in range(3):
                arr = dp[last]
                for sc in range(size):
                    val = arr[sc]
                    if val == 0:
                        continue
                    for m in nxt[last]:
                        ns = sc + cur[m]
                        # ns is always in bounds
                        nv = ndp[m][ns] + val
                        if nv >= MOD:
                            nv -= MOD
                        ndp[m][ns] = nv
            dp = ndp
        
        ans = 0
        for m in range(3):
            ans += sum(dp[m][shift + 1:])
        return ans % MOD
```

Sum < 2MOD. subtract once yields < MOD. Good. But what about if ndp[m][ns] was updated earlier in same round and after subtract once remains < MOD. yes. Good.

Let's maybe consider if there is a possibility to optimize by using `for sc, val in enumerate(arr):` instead of `range(size)`; either. `enumerate` maybe faster. Use `for sc, val in enumerate(arr):` and if val. Then ns = sc + cur[m]. Good. Let's use enumerate for readability.

Code:

```python
class Solution:
    def countWinningSequences(self, s: str) -> int:
        MOD = 1_000_000_007
        n = len(s)
        shift = n
        size = 2 * n + 3

        inc = {
            'F': [0, 1, -1],
            'W': [-1, 0, 1],
            'E': [1, -1, 0],
        }
        nxt = ((1, 2), (0, 2), (0, 1))

        dp = [[0] * size for _ in range(3)]
        first = inc[s[0]]
        for m in range(3):
            dp[m][shift + first[m]] = 1

        for ch in s[1:]:
            cur = inc[ch]
            ndp = [[0] * size for _ in range(3)]
            for last in range(3):
                arr = dp[last]
                for sc, val in enumerate(arr):
                    if not val:
                        continue
                    for m in nxt[last]:
                        ns = sc + cur[m]
                        nv = ndp[m][ns] + val
                        if nv >= MOD:
                            nv -= MOD
                        ndp[m][ns] = nv
            dp = ndp

        ans = 0
        for m in range(3):
            ans += sum(dp[m][shift + 1:])
        return ans % MOD
```

Let's maybe think if there is any issue with "n=1000" and "size=2003" but if `ns
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
class Solution:
    def countWinningSequences(self, s: str) -> int:
        MOD = 1_000_000_007
        n = len(s)
        shift = n
        size = 2 * n + 3

        # Index order: 0 = F, 1 = W, 2 = E
        inc = {
            'F': [0, 1, -1],
            'W': [-1, 0, 1],
            'E': [1, -1, 0],
        }
        nxt = ((1, 2), (0, 2), (0, 1))

        dp = [[0] * size for _ in range(3)]
        first = inc[s[0]]
        for m in range(3):
            dp[m][shift + first[m]] = 1

        for ch in s[1:]:
            cur = inc[ch]
            ndp = [[0] * size for _ in range(3)]

            for last in range(3):
                arr = dp[last]
                for sc, val in enumerate(arr):
                    if not val:
                        continue
                    for m in nxt[last]:
                        ns = sc + cur[m]
                        nv = ndp[m][ns] + val
                        if nv >= MOD:
                            nv -= MOD
                        ndp[m][ns] = nv

            dp = ndp

        ans = 0
        for m in range(3):
            ans += sum(dp[m][shift + 1:])

        return ans % MOD
```
